Well, I’m here because… it’s this work contract, the environmental modeling one. I’ve been doing revisions on the datasets, and I’m pretty sure some people involved are tracking what I change and then slipping errors back in so it looks like I can’t manage the project.

My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control problems, but I know what ordinary mistakes look like. No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.

The environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.

So outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.

Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.

But no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.

I mean, that environmental modeling work has a lot of moving parts, and if someone knows the workflow, they can track a revision and make a change look like an accident.\n\nMy wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts keep saying that too, but I’ve been doing this kind of data work long enough to know when errors don’t just happen. So… plotting against me in general? No, not exactly. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. But now… no, not really. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because… it’s this work contract, the environmental modeling one. I’ve been doing revisions on the datasets, and I’m pretty sure some people involved are tracking what I change and then slipping errors back in so it looks like I can’t manage the project.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control problems, but I know what ordinary mistakes look like. I’ve worked with data long enough to tell when something’s been… nudged, you know? And now it’s affecting whether people think I can lead the work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been thinking that people are intentionally slipping errors into your work?\n\nPatient: About two months, I’d say. I mean, that’s when it stopped feeling like normal messy version-control stuff and more like someone was watching my revisions and putting things back wrong on purpose. It’s hard to explain without getting into the whole dataset structure, but… yeah, the certainty of it has been there maybe two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like people are watching you or plotting against you outside of this work project?\n\nPatient: Outside of the project… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. It’s really centered on the contract. I mean, that environmental modeling work has a lot of moving parts, and if someone knows the workflow, they can track a revision and make a change look like an accident.\n\nMy wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts keep saying that too, but I’ve been doing this kind of data work long enough to know when errors don’t just happen. So… plotting against me in general? No, not exactly. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. But now… no, not really. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.

It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because… it’s this work contract, the environmental modeling one. I’ve been doing revisions on the datasets, and I’m pretty sure some people involved are tracking what I change and then slipping errors back in so it looks like I can’t manage the project.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control problems, but I know what ordinary mistakes look like. I’ve worked with data long enough to tell when something’s been… nudged, you know? And now it’s affecting whether people think I can lead the work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been thinking that people are intentionally slipping errors into your work?\n\nPatient: About two months, I’d say. I mean, that’s when it stopped feeling like normal messy version-control stuff and more like someone was watching my revisions and putting things back wrong on purpose. It’s hard to explain without getting into the whole dataset structure, but… yeah, the certainty of it has been there maybe two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like people are watching you or plotting against you outside of this work project?\n\nPatient: Outside of the project… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. It’s really centered on the contract. I mean, that environmental modeling work has a lot of moving parts, and if someone knows the workflow, they can track a revision and make a change look like an accident.\n\nMy wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts keep saying that too, but I’ve been doing this kind of data work long enough to know when errors don’t just happen. So… plotting against me in general? No, not exactly. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I’ve been doing revisions on the datasets, and I’m pretty sure some people involved are tracking what I change and then slipping errors back in so it looks like I can’t manage the project.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control problems, but I know what ordinary mistakes look like. I’ve worked with data long enough to tell when something’s been… nudged, you know? It’s really centered on the contract. I mean, that environmental modeling work has a lot of moving parts, and if someone knows the workflow, they can track a revision and make a change look like an accident.\n\nMy wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts keep saying that too, but I’ve been doing this kind of data work long enough to know when errors don’t just happen. So… plotting against me in general? No, not exactly. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. But now… no, not really. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. The environmental modeling work. I’m pretty sure people there were tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it would look like I couldn’t lead it. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but… no, that doesn’t explain it. So if you mean plotting, that’s where I see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your revisions on the environmental modeling project could be incorrect, even if you're sure someone is sabotaging you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I used to tell the younger analysts that, actually, back when we’d have these long meetings and everyone would bring coffee and pretend coffee solved bad data.\n\nBut this isn’t just my revisions being wrong. I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that contract were tracking what I changed and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it would look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife says, “It’s version-control mistakes,” and the junior analysts say the same thing, but it keeps happening in a pattern. So yes, I can check my work, but that doesn’t explain what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you completely stopped believing someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped believing it? No… not completely. I’ve had moments where I tried to set it aside, like when my wife would say, “You’re getting too wrapped up in this,” and I’d go make tea or reorganize the files—though reorganizing files is how I lose half a Saturday, honestly.\n\nBut the belief doesn’t really go away. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract were tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets to make me look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control mistakes, but that doesn’t feel right to me. So… no, I can’t say there was a time I completely stopped believing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Concrete evidence… I mean, not like a signed note saying “we sabotaged the dataset,” no. People don’t leave things like that lying around, especially not on a contract where everyone knows revisions are tracked.

Changed? Not really… I mean, some days I can distract myself. I’ll look at the files, then end up thinking about something else for a while—like how the old modeling software used to crash right before lunch, and everyone blamed the server room temperature, which was ridiculous because nobody ever checked the simplest things.

If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. The environmental modeling work. I’m pretty sure people there were tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it would look like I couldn’t lead it. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but… no, that doesn’t explain it. So if you mean plotting, that’s where I see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your revisions on the environmental modeling project could be incorrect, even if you're sure someone is sabotaging you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I used to tell the younger analysts that, actually, back when we’d have these long meetings and everyone would bring coffee and pretend coffee solved bad data.\n\nBut this isn’t just my revisions being wrong. I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that contract were tracking what I changed and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it would look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife says, “It’s version-control mistakes,” and the junior analysts say the same thing, but it keeps happening in a pattern. So yes, I can check my work, but that doesn’t explain what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you completely stopped believing someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped believing it? No… not completely. I’ve had moments where I tried to set it aside, like when my wife would say, “You’re getting too wrapped up in this,” and I’d go make tea or reorganize the files—though reorganizing files is how I lose half a Saturday, honestly.\n\nBut the belief doesn’t really go away. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract were tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets to make me look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control mistakes, but that doesn’t feel right to me. So… no, I can’t say there was a time I completely stopped believing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found any concrete evidence that proves someone is intentionally sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… I mean, not like a signed note saying “we sabotaged the dataset,” no. People don’t leave things like that lying around, especially not on a contract where everyone knows revisions are tracked.\n\nBut I’ve seen enough in the files. I’d make a correction, and then later the error would be back in there, or something adjacent would be off. And when I’d ask about it, the junior analysts would say it was version-control confusion, same as my wife says. But version-control mistakes don’t keep lining up in a way that makes me look like I can’t manage the model.\n\nSorry, I know I’m circling. The short answer is I don’t have one single piece of proof you’d call concrete. But I’m pretty sure people were tracking my revisions and planting errors back into the datasets.\n\nDoctor: Has your belief that someone is sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or weakened over the two months you've been thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Changed? Not really… I mean, some days I can distract myself. I’ll look at the files, then end up thinking about something else for a while—like how the old modeling software used to crash right before lunch, and everyone blamed the server room temperature, which was ridiculous because nobody ever checked the simplest things.\n\nBut no, the main belief hasn’t really weakened. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets, so it looks like I can’t lead the work. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but it still feels too patterned to be accidental. Not really… I mean, some days I can distract myself. I’ll look at the files, then end up thinking about something else for a while—like how the old modeling software used to crash right before lunch, and everyone blamed the server room temperature, which was ridiculous because nobody ever checked the simplest things.\n\nBut no, the main belief hasn’t really weakened. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets, so it looks like I can’t lead the work. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but it still feels too patterned to be accidental. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Other areas? I… I don’t think so, not really. It’s mostly tied to the environmental modeling contract. That’s where the pattern is.

I mean, I can get suspicious in general when people are sloppy with files—because one bad filename can ruin your whole afternoon, and nobody respects folder structure anymore—but the thing I’m talking about, the tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets, that’s about that project. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s version-control mistakes, but it doesn’t feel like just mistakes to me.

So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s spread everywhere. It’s mainly the work on that model.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because… it’s this work contract, the environmental modeling one. I’ve been doing revisions on the datasets, and I’m pretty sure some people involved are tracking what I change and then slipping errors back in so it looks like I can’t manage the project.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control problems, but I know what ordinary mistakes look like. I’ve worked with data long enough to tell when something’s been… nudged, you know? And now it’s affecting whether people think I can lead the work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been thinking that people are intentionally slipping errors into your work?\n\nPatient: About two months, I’d say. I mean, that’s when it stopped feeling like normal messy version-control stuff and more like someone was watching my revisions and putting things back wrong on purpose. It’s hard to explain without getting into the whole dataset structure, but… yeah, the certainty of it has been there maybe two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like people are watching you or plotting against you outside of this work project?\n\nPatient: Outside of the project… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. It’s really centered on the contract. I mean, that environmental modeling work has a lot of moving parts, and if someone knows the workflow, they can track a revision and make a change look like an accident.\n\nMy wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts keep saying that too, but I’ve been doing this kind of data work long enough to know when errors don’t just happen. So… plotting against me in general? No, not exactly. But on this project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re setting it up so it looks like I can’t lead it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, even for a short time, where you didn't think someone was intentionally sabotaging your work on this project?\n\nPatient: Maybe at the very beginning, before I saw the pattern, I could tell myself it was just the files getting crossed up. But now… no, not really. Once I noticed how the revisions were being tracked and then the dataset would come back with these errors planted in it, it’s hard to unsee that.\n\nAnd I know my wife says it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t know the structure like I do. I’ve led models before—well, not always officially led, but I’ve been the person people come to when the numbers don’t line up—and this doesn’t feel random. So, no, I haven’t really had a break from thinking someone on that contract is trying to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are out to get you or sabotaging you in any area of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like… the neighbors or people at the grocery store or anything. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. The environmental modeling work. I’m pretty sure people there were tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it would look like I couldn’t lead it. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but… no, that doesn’t explain it. So if you mean plotting, that’s where I see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your revisions on the environmental modeling project could be incorrect, even if you're sure someone is sabotaging you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I used to tell the younger analysts that, actually, back when we’d have these long meetings and everyone would bring coffee and pretend coffee solved bad data.\n\nBut this isn’t just my revisions being wrong. I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that contract were tracking what I changed and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it would look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife says, “It’s version-control mistakes,” and the junior analysts say the same thing, but it keeps happening in a pattern. So yes, I can check my work, but that doesn’t explain what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you completely stopped believing someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped believing it? No… not completely. I’ve had moments where I tried to set it aside, like when my wife would say, “You’re getting too wrapped up in this,” and I’d go make tea or reorganize the files—though reorganizing files is how I lose half a Saturday, honestly.\n\nBut the belief doesn’t really go away. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract were tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets to make me look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control mistakes, but that doesn’t feel right to me. So… no, I can’t say there was a time I completely stopped believing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found any concrete evidence that proves someone is intentionally sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… I mean, not like a signed note saying “we sabotaged the dataset,” no. People don’t leave things like that lying around, especially not on a contract where everyone knows revisions are tracked.\n\nBut I’ve seen enough in the files. I’d make a correction, and then later the error would be back in there, or something adjacent would be off. And when I’d ask about it, the junior analysts would say it was version-control confusion, same as my wife says. But version-control mistakes don’t keep lining up in a way that makes me look like I can’t manage the model.\n\nSorry, I know I’m circling. The short answer is I don’t have one single piece of proof you’d call concrete. But I’m pretty sure people were tracking my revisions and planting errors back into the datasets.\n\nDoctor: Has your belief that someone is sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or weakened over the two months you've been thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Changed? I… I don’t think so, not really. It’s mostly tied to the environmental modeling contract. It’s mainly the work on that model.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… in theory, sure, if someone showed me exactly what happened in the version control, step by step, maybe I’d have to look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable.

But the problem is, people keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, and it doesn’t. I’ve worked with messy files before—years ago we had a whole model run thrown off because someone named two folders almost the same thing, and nobody noticed until the meeting, which was embarrassing but it was just sloppy. This feels different.

I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it looks like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s probably mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing, but… it keeps lining up too neatly.

So could it change? Maybe if the new information was very clear. But right now, I don’t really see what would explain the pattern any other way.

I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. It’s not that. It’s this contract.\n\nThe environmental modeling work has all these revisions, and people think that makes it easy to explain away mistakes, but that’s exactly the problem. If someone is tracking what I change and then putting errors back into the datasets, it makes me look like I can’t keep the model straight. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but they don’t see the whole structure the way I do.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not really. But on that project, yes, I’m pretty sure they’re sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts about someone sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or wavered since you first started suspecting it?\n\nPatient: It maybe… at first I tried to talk myself out of it, because with environmental modeling there are always a hundred file versions, and I’ve seen people mess up naming conventions. I used to keep folders for everything—my wife says I keep too many folders, actually, she jokes the computer has more organization than the garage.\n\nBut no, once I saw the revisions being tracked and the errors showing back up in the datasets, it hasn’t really wavered much. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but that doesn’t fit what I’m seeing. It feels pretty clear to me that someone on that contract is planting those errors so it looks like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project are targeting you specifically, or is it just about the project itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s me, mostly. I mean, the project is the setting, sure, because that’s where the datasets are and all the revisions, but… they’re tracking my changes, not just the project in some general way.\n\nIt’s like when I was younger and we had this one filing cabinet at work—different job, not environmental modeling—and everyone blamed the cabinet when things went missing, but it was always the same person’s folders that got moved. That’s the kind of thing I mean. People say “version-control mistakes,” my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but the errors show up after my revisions. So it feels aimed at making me look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m busy with something else and I’m not actively thinking about it. Like if I’m fixing something around the house, or my wife has me going through old boxes—she keeps saying I saved every cable since 1998, which is probably true.\n\nBut a whole day? No, not really. Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. The environmental modeling work. I’m pretty sure people there were tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it would look like I couldn’t lead it. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but… no, that doesn’t explain it. So if you mean plotting, that’s where I see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your revisions on the environmental modeling project could be incorrect, even if you're sure someone is sabotaging you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I used to tell the younger analysts that, actually, back when we’d have these long meetings and everyone would bring coffee and pretend coffee solved bad data.\n\nBut this isn’t just my revisions being wrong. I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that contract were tracking what I changed and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it would look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife says, “It’s version-control mistakes,” and the junior analysts say the same thing, but it keeps happening in a pattern. So yes, I can check my work, but that doesn’t explain what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you completely stopped believing someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped believing it? No… not completely. I’ve had moments where I tried to set it aside, like when my wife would say, “You’re getting too wrapped up in this,” and I’d go make tea or reorganize the files—though reorganizing files is how I lose half a Saturday, honestly.\n\nBut the belief doesn’t really go away. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract were tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets to make me look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control mistakes, but that doesn’t feel right to me. So… no, I can’t say there was a time I completely stopped believing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found any concrete evidence that proves someone is intentionally sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… I mean, not like a signed note saying “we sabotaged the dataset,” no. People don’t leave things like that lying around, especially not on a contract where everyone knows revisions are tracked.\n\nBut I’ve seen enough in the files. I’d make a correction, and then later the error would be back in there, or something adjacent would be off. And when I’d ask about it, the junior analysts would say it was version-control confusion, same as my wife says. But version-control mistakes don’t keep lining up in a way that makes me look like I can’t manage the model.\n\nSorry, I know I’m circling. It’s mainly the work on that model.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project could change based on new information?\n\nPatient: I mean… in theory, sure, if someone showed me exactly what happened in the version control, step by step, maybe I’d have to look at it. If it really was shown to me clearly, maybe… I’d have to consider it. I’m not saying machines never mess things up. Version control can be a nightmare—half the time people don’t even write useful commit notes, just “updates” or “fixed stuff,” which tells you nothing.

But that’s what bothers me. Everyone keeps saying it’s just a technical issue or version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets. It makes me look like I can’t lead the work.

So… yes, if there was proof, maybe. But right now it doesn’t feel like some random system glitch. It feels too deliberate.

Once I saw the way my revisions were being tracked and then the errors would show back up in the datasets, it’s hard to just not think that. People keep saying version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but it doesn’t explain why it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. So no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I didn’t believe someone on that contract was doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people sabotaging your work could be just making mistakes or is it definitely on purpose?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s just mistakes. I know people keep saying that—my wife says version-control mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing—but it’s too consistent. The errors come back after my revisions, in a way that makes it look like I can’t lead the work.\n\nI mean, sure, in modeling you get messy files, that happens. I’ve made my share of folders and backup folders—probably too many, if you ask my wife—but this doesn’t feel like ordinary confusion. It feels like someone on that contract is tracking what I change and planting the errors on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about someone sabotaging your work could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I mean… I suppose anything could be wrong in theory. I’m not saying I’ve got a courtroom proof sitting in a folder—though I do have folders, too many, my wife says the whole office looks like a paper recycling plant.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I’m wrong about this. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it makes me look like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but that explanation doesn’t fit what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were out to get you or plotting against you in a situation that didn't involve work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t know… I mean, people can be difficult anywhere, neighbors with fences and noise and all that, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that feels real to me is the contract. The environmental modeling work. I’m pretty sure people there were tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it would look like I couldn’t lead it. My wife keeps saying it’s version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but… no, that doesn’t explain it. So if you mean plotting, that’s where I see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your revisions on the environmental modeling project could be incorrect, even if you're sure someone is sabotaging you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it in the way you check your own math, sure. I mean, environmental models are not simple—one misplaced assumption and the whole output looks strange. I used to tell the younger analysts that, actually, back when we’d have these long meetings and everyone would bring coffee and pretend coffee solved bad data.\n\nBut this isn’t just my revisions being wrong. I’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that contract were tracking what I changed and then putting errors back into the datasets, so it would look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife says, “It’s version-control mistakes,” and the junior analysts say the same thing, but it keeps happening in a pattern. So yes, I can check my work, but that doesn’t explain what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you completely stopped believing someone was sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped believing it? No… not completely. I’ve had moments where I tried to set it aside, like when my wife would say, “You’re getting too wrapped up in this,” and I’d go make tea or reorganize the files—though reorganizing files is how I lose half a Saturday, honestly.\n\nBut the belief doesn’t really go away. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract were tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets to make me look like I couldn’t lead the work. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s just version-control mistakes, but that doesn’t feel right to me. So… no, I can’t say there was a time I completely stopped believing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found any concrete evidence that proves someone is intentionally sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Concrete evidence… I mean, not like a signed note saying “we sabotaged the dataset,” no. People don’t leave things like that lying around, especially not on a contract where everyone knows revisions are tracked.\n\nBut I’ve seen enough in the files. I’d make a correction, and then later the error would be back in there, or something adjacent would be off. And when I’d ask about it, the junior analysts would say it was version-control confusion, same as my wife says. But version-control mistakes don’t keep lining up in a way that makes me look like I can’t manage the model.\n\nSorry, I know I’m circling. The short answer is I don’t have one single piece of proof you’d call concrete. But I’m pretty sure people were tracking my revisions and planting errors back into the datasets.\n\nDoctor: Has your belief that someone is sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project ever changed or weakened over the two months you've been thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Changed? Not really… I mean, some days I can distract myself. I’ll look at the files, then end up thinking about something else for a while—like how the old modeling software used to crash right before lunch, and everyone blamed the server room temperature, which was ridiculous because nobody ever checked the simplest things.\n\nBut no, the main belief hasn’t really weakened. I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets, so it looks like I can’t lead the work. My wife keeps saying it’s just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say that too, but it still feels too patterned to be accidental. So… I wouldn’t say it’s changed much over the two months.\n\nDoctor: Has your suspicion of being sabotaged started to apply to other areas of your life beyond the environmental modeling project?\n\nPatient: Other areas? I… I don’t think so, not really. It’s mostly tied to the environmental modeling contract. That’s where the pattern is.\n\nI mean, I can get suspicious in general when people are sloppy with files—because one bad filename can ruin your whole afternoon, and nobody respects folder structure anymore—but the thing I’m talking about, the tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets, that’s about that project. My wife and the junior analysts keep saying it’s version-control mistakes, but it doesn’t feel like just mistakes to me.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say it’s spread everywhere. It’s mainly the work on that model.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about people sabotaging your work on the environmental modeling project could change based on new information?\n\nPatient: I mean… in theory, sure, if someone showed me exactly what happened in the version control, step by step, maybe I’d have to look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable.\n\nBut the problem is, people keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, and it doesn’t. I’ve worked with messy files before—years ago we had a whole model run thrown off because someone named two folders almost the same thing, and nobody noticed until the meeting, which was embarrassing but it was just sloppy. This feels different.\n\nI’m here because I’m pretty sure people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and putting errors back into the datasets so it looks like I can’t lead the work. My wife says it’s probably mistakes, the junior analysts say the same thing, but… it keeps lining up too neatly.\n\nSo could it change? Maybe if the new information was very clear. But right now, I don’t really see what would explain the pattern any other way.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider changing your mind about someone sabotaging your work if you found out it was actually a technical issue with the version control system?\n\nPatient: If it really was shown to me clearly, maybe… I’d have to consider it. I’m not saying machines never mess things up. Version control can be a nightmare—half the time people don’t even write useful commit notes, just “updates” or “fixed stuff,” which tells you nothing.\n\nBut that’s what bothers me. Everyone keeps saying it’s just a technical issue or version-control mistakes, my wife says that, the junior analysts say that, but I’m here because I’m pretty sure the people on that environmental modeling contract have been tracking my revisions and planting errors in the datasets.
